On the congruence ideal associated to p p -adic families of Yoshida lifts
Ming-Lun Hsieh and Bharathwaj Palvannan

Abstract
. We study congruences involving p p -adic families of Hecke eigensystems of Yoshida lifts associated with two Hida families (say ℱ , 𝒢 \mathcal{F},\mathcal{G} ) of elliptic cusp forms. With appropriate hypotheses, we show that if a Hida family of genus two Siegel cusp forms admits a Yoshida lift at an appropriately chosen classical specialization, then all classical specializations are Yoshida lifts. Moreover, we prove that the characteristic ideal of the non-primitive Selmer group of (a self-dual twist of) the Rankin–Selberg convolution of ℱ \mathcal{F} and 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is divisible by the congruence ideal of the Yoshida lift associated with ℱ \mathcal{F} and 𝒢 \mathcal{G} . Under an additional assumption inspired by pseudo-nullity conjectures in higher codimension Iwasawa theory, we establish the pseudo-cyclicity of the dual of the primitive Selmer group over the cyclotomic ℤ p \mathbb{Z}_{p} -extension.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

The idea to study congruences between Eisenstein series of
\mathrm{GL}(2)
over
\mathbb{Q}
and cuspidal eigenforms dates back to Ribet’s proof of the converse to Herbrand’s theorem. The idea can in fact be traced to earlier unpublished calculations of Greenberg and Monsky. Generalizations of this idea have been applied in Iwasawa theory with tremendous success, most notably first by Mazur–Wiles to prove the Iwasawa main conjecture over
\mathbb{Q}
. The space of Yoshida lifts of a pair of primitive Hida families
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
of elliptic modular forms form the appropriate analog of the Eisenstein series in our study of
2
-variable Iwasawa conjecture involving the Rankin–Selberg product of two Hida families
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
. The genesis of the construction of Yoshida lifts of cuspforms goes back to Yoshida
[
90
,
91
]
. There are generalizations of this construction in works of Böcherer–Schulze-Pillot
[
13
,
14
]
. We are interested in studying the
p
-adic family of Hecke eigensystems corresponding to the space of Yoshida lifts of
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
. We rely on works of Hida
[
40
]
and Pilloni
[
69
]
on Hida theory for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
over
\mathbb{Q}
involving coherent cohomology.

Our first result is a type of rigidity result proved under (among others) an assumption that amounts to the finiteness of the Rankin-Selberg Selmer group (as predicted by the Bloch–Kato conjecture) for suitably chosen classical specializations
(f_{0},g_{0})
of the pair of primitive Hida families
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
. We prove that a
p
-adic family of non-primitive Hecke eigensystems of
\mathrm{GSp}(4)
over
\mathbb{Q}
that contains the Hecke eigensystem corresponding to the space of Yoshida lifts of
(f_{0},g_{0})
is unique and, in fact, corresponds to the
p
-adic family of Hecke eigensystems associated to the space of Yoshida lifts of the Hida families
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
. Furthermore, we prove that the Galois representation associated to a branch of the
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
ordinary Hecke algebra is irreducible if the branch does not correspond to the Hecke eigensystem of Yoshida lifts of Hida families. These results also crucially use the modularity results of Diamond
[
18
]
and Skinner–Wiles
[
79
]
. See Theorem
1
for the precise statement.

The idea to study congruences between cuspidal eigenforms of
\mathrm{GL}(2)
over
\mathbb{Q}
, on the other hand, dates back to an unpublished manuscript of Doi–Hida. This topic was then studied in a series of articles by Hida in the early 1980s. Hida
[
37
]
introduced the general notion of a
congruence module
— which is a module over the Hecke algebra — to systematically keep track of these congruences. When applied to study congruences between
p
-adic families of Eisenstein series of
\mathrm{GL}(2)
over
\mathbb{Q}
and cuspidal eigenforms, the congruence module inherits an additional structure over Iwasawa algebra. It turns out to be isomorphic to a quotient of the Iwasawa algebra by the
congruence ideal
in the Iwasawa algebra. Under some assumptions, Emerton
[
20
]
(in level one) and Ohta
[
66
]
(for a general level) have showed that the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function generates the congruence ideal. The interplay between the Hecke-module structure and the Iwasawa-module structure of the congruence module along with the subsequent link on the analytic side between the congruence ideal and the
p
-adic
L
-function is an important motivation for us to pursue this generalization and study the congruence ideal and the congruence module associated to
p
-adic families of Yoshida lifts and their applications to the Iwasawa–Greenberg main conjecture for Rankin–Selberg product of Hida families. See Theorem
2
and Corollary
1.13
for the precise statements.

Our application of congruence ideals to main conjectures is inspired by works of Hida–Tilouine
[
46
,
47
]
towards anti-cyclotomic main conjectures for CM fields, where they study congruences involving CM forms. The idea to use Yoshida lifts to support the Bloch–Kato conjecture for the Rankin–Selberg product of cusp forms dates back to works of Agarwal–Klosin
[
2
]
and Böcherer–Dummigan–Schulze-Pillot
[
8
]
. Our third main source of inspiration is work of Urban on the Eisenstein-Klingen ideal
[
84
]
.

Ohta
[
65
]
, generalizing works of Harder–Pink
[
45
]
and Kurihara
[
52
]
, was able to provide an alternative proof of the Iwasawa main conjecture over
\mathbb{Q}
. Ohta’s proof allowed him to obtain finer information about the structure of the classical Iwasawa module over both the Iwasawa algebra and the Hecke algebra. Ohta was also able to formulate hypotheses (see for example
[
67
, Theorem 2]
) under which the classical Iwasawa module can be shown to be a cyclic module over the Iwasawa algebra. However, Wake
[
86
]
has shown that these hypotheses need not always hold and that the Iwasawa module need not always be cyclic over the Iwasawa algebra. One is then naturally led to ask whether there are a natural set of hypotheses under which the classical Iwasawa module can be shown to be pseudo-cyclic over the Iwasawa algebra. Wake
[
86
]
obtained such results on pseudo-cyclicity of the Iwasawa module under hypotheses linked to pseudo-nullity conjectures of Greenberg for totally real abelian number fields. We prove pseudo-cyclicity of the dual of the primitive Selmer group associated to the cyclotomic deformation of the Rankin–Selberg product of Hida families under an assumption inspired by pseudo-nullity conjectures in higher codimension Iwasawa theory. See Theorem
3
for the precise statement.

1.2.
Hida theory for elliptic modular forms

Let
p
be an odd prime. Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be the ring of integers in a finite suitably large extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We fix once and for an isomorphism
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\cong\mathbb{C}
and embeddings
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\cong\mathbb{C}
.
Denote by
\omega:(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{\times}\to\mathbb{\mu}_{p-1}
the mod-p character and also view
\omega
as a Dirichlet character modulo
p
via
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. Let

\mathcal{F}=\sum_{n>0}a_{n}(\mathcal{F})q^{n}\in{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}[[q]];\quad\mathcal{G}=\sum_{n>0}a_{n}(\mathcal{G})q^{n}\in{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}[[q]]

be two primitive Hida families of elliptic modular forms with the tame conductors
N_{\mathcal{F}}
and
N_{\mathcal{G}}
.
Throughout this manuscript, we shall impose the following conditions:

Hypothesis 1.1
.

(
Irr)

As
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-representations, the residual representations associated to
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
respectively (say
\overline{\rho}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\mathcal{G}}
) are irreducible.

(
p
-Dist)

The restrictions to
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
of the semi-simplifications of the residual representations
\overline{\rho}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\mathcal{G}}
are non-scalar.

(
Mult-Free)

As
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
-representations, the residual representations
\overline{\rho}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\mathcal{G}}
are non-isomorphic.

Let
\Sigma
denote the set of primes in
\mathbb{Q}
containing
p
,
\infty
and the the primes dividing
N_{\mathcal{F}}
and
N_{\mathcal{G}}
. Let
\Sigma_{0}=\Sigma\setminus\{p\}
. Let
\mathbb{Q}_{\Sigma}
denote the maximal extension of
\mathbb{Q}
unramified outside
\Sigma
. Let
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}
denote the Galois group of the cyclotomic
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-extension
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{cyc}}
over
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{F},N_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{G},N_{\mathcal{G}}}
denote Hida’s ordinary Hecke algebra passing through
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
with tame levels
N_{\mathcal{F}}
and
N_{\mathcal{G}}
respectively. Let
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}
denote the integral closures in the fraction fields of the irreducible components passing through
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
respectively. The Hecke algebras consist of the Hecke operators
T_{l}
and the normalized diamond operators
\langle l\rangle
at good primes
l\neq p
along with the
U_{p}
operator. The rings
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}
are finite extensions of the one variable power series rings
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![x_{\mathcal{F}}]\!]
and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![x_{\mathcal{G}}]\!]
respectively, where
x_{\mathcal{F}}
and
x_{\mathcal{G}}
are the weight variables for the Hida families. We will assume that the integral closure of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}
are equal (to
{\mathcal{O}}
, extending scalars if necessary).

We let

\rho_{\mathcal{F}}:\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}),\quad\rho_{\mathcal{G}}:\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}})

denote the Galois representations constructed by Hida
[
36
]
associated to
F
and
G
respectively. We let
\mathrm{L}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathrm{L}_{\mathcal{G}}
denote the corresponding free modules, associated to these Galois representations, of rank two over
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}
respectively. It is possible to factor the characters

\det(\rho_{\mathcal{F}}):G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}[\![x_{\mathcal{F}}]\!]^{\times},\quad\det(\rho_{\mathcal{G}}):G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}[\![x_{\mathcal{G}}]\!]^{\times}

as the products

(1)

\displaystyle\det(\rho_{\mathcal{F}})=\chi_{\mathcal{F}}\omega^{k_{f_{0}}}\varepsilon^{-1}\kappa_{\mathcal{F}},\qquad\det(\rho_{\mathcal{G}})=\chi_{\mathcal{G}}\omega^{k_{g_{0}}}\varepsilon^{-1}\kappa_{\mathcal{G}},

where the characters
\kappa_{\mathcal{F}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}[\![x_{\mathcal{F}}]\!]^{\times}
,
\kappa_{\mathcal{G}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}[\![x_{\mathcal{G}}]\!]^{\times}
are the tautological characters that factor through the pro-
p
group
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}
, whereas
\chi_{\mathcal{F}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
are finite Dirichlet characters of order and conductor prime to
p
, whereas
\omega:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
is the mod-
p
cyclotomic character. The character
\varepsilon:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
is the
p
-adic cyclotomic character. The integers
k_{f_{0}}
and
k_{g_{0}}
are the weights of specializations
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
, that are chosen in the next subsection.

Let
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
denote the normalization of the completed tensor product
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}\hat{\otimes}{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}
over
{\mathcal{O}}
. Note that we have natural inclusions
i_{F}:{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
and
i_{G}:{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
. Let
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
be the usual Iwasawa algebra over
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
. This topological
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
-algebra is isomorphic to the power series ring
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![x]\!]
, the isomorphism being (non-canonically) defined by sending a chosen topological generator
\gamma_{0}
of
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}
to
x+1
. Consider the tautological character

\widetilde{\kappa}:G_{\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]^{\times}.

Let
\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}:\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{4}\left({{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}\llbracket{\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}}\rrbracket}\right)
denote the Galois representation obtained by the action of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
on the following
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
-module of rank four:

\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}}}\bigg(\mathrm{L}_{F}\otimes_{{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}}{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}},\ \mathrm{L}_{G}\otimes_{{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}\bigg)\otimes_{{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}}{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!](\widetilde{\kappa}^{-1}).

Remark 1.2
.

We impose the condition that the residual representations associated to
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are irreducible along with the hypothesis

p

-Dist
so that the Galois representations
\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}
and
\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}
(introduced in equation
3
) satisfy Greenberg’s Panchishkin condition which in turn allows us to formulate the Iwasawa–Greenberg main conjecture in the Rankin–Selberg setting. Furthermore the hypothesis
Irr
, that the residual
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-representations associated to
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are irreducible, allows us apply
R^{\mathrm{ord}}=T
theorems of Skinner–Wiles
[
79
]
(which is a strengthening of a theorem of Diamond
[
18
]
). The hypothesis
Mult-Free
plays a key role while using results of Bellaıche–Chenevier
[
6
]
on pseudocharacters.

1.3.
Iwasawa–Greenberg main conjectures in the Rankin–Selberg setting

We consider below Conjectures
1.3
and
1.7
that fall under the umbrella of Iwasawa–Greenberg main conjectures. Depending on the choice of the dominant Hida family, the Galois representation
\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}
satisfies Greenberg’s Panchishkin condition
[
26
]
. We will usually denote our choice of dominant Hida family using an underlined font. Without loss of generality, let us consider the case when the dominant Hida family is
\mathcal{F}
. One can, in fact, consider a non-primitive
3
-variable Rankin–Selberg
p
-adic
L
-function
\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{4},3}
– also constructed by Hida
[
35
,
38
,
39
]
— in the fraction field of
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
. See work of Dasgupta
[
17
]
or work of the first author and Chen
[
16
]
for a construction of the primitive
p
-adic
L
-function. For these
3
-variable
p
-adic
L
-functions, in addition to the weight variables of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, one can vary the cyclotomic variable
s
. On the algebraic side, we have a discrete non-primitive Selmer group
\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}}(\mathbb{Q})
, whose Pontryagin dual is a finitely generated
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
-module.

Conjecture 1.3

(Conjecture 4.1 in
[
26
]
)
.

The
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
-module
\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{F}}},\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}
is torsion. Furthermore, we have the following equality of diviors of
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
:

(2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee})=\mathrm{Div}(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{4},3}}).

Lei–Loeffler–Zerbes
[
56
]
and Loeffler–Kings–Zerbes
[
51
,
50
]
are able to utilize the Euler systems of Beilinson–Flach elements to deduce, under suitable assumptions, the finiteness of the Rankin–Selberg Selmer groups. This motivates the hypothesis that we introduce below — labeled
Tor
.

There exists classical specializations
f_{0}
,
g_{0}
of the Hida families
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, with level prime to
p
, satisfying the following conditions:

Hypothesis 1.4
.

(
Tor)

The Rankin–Selberg Selmer group associated to
f_{0}
,
g_{0}
over
\mathbb{Q}
is finite.

(
CT)

\mathrm{weight}(f_{0})-\mathrm{weight}(g_{0})\geq 4
.

(
Yos-Det)

\mathrm{weight}(f_{0})\equiv\mathrm{weight}(g_{0})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2(p-1))
.

(
Yos-disc)

There exists a finite prime
\ell_{0}\neq p
such that
\pi_{f_{0},\ell_{0}}
and
\pi_{g_{0},\ell_{0}}
— the irreducible admissible representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell_{0}})
naturally associated to the cusp forms
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
(in the sense of
[
24
, Definition 6.1]
) — are both discrete series representations (in the sense of
[
9
, Section 17.4]
).

We also need the following hypothesis, which is necessary when one wants to consider Yoshida lifts of cusp forms:

(
Yos-Neb)

\chi_{F}=\chi_{G}
.

One of our primary goals is to obtain a partial result towards the inequality

\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee})\geq\mathrm{Div}(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{4},3}}),

by studying
p
-adic families of Yoshida lifts.

Remark 1.5
.

All of the hypotheses
Yos-Neb
,
Yos-Det
and
Yos-disc
are required for the construction of Yoshida lifts. See Section
3.2
. The local representation
\pi_{f,\ell_{0}}
participates in the local Jacquet–Langlands correspondence for
\mathrm{GL}(2)
precisely when
\pi_{f,\ell_{0}}
is a discrete series
[
9
, Section 56]
. The hypothesis
Yos-disc
is equivalent to the fact that there exists at least one finite prime
\ell_{0}
such that both
\pi_{f_{0},\ell_{0}}
and
\pi_{g_{0},\ell_{0}}
participate in the local Jacquet–Langlands correspondence for
\mathrm{GL}(2)
, which is then required when applying Arthur’s multiplicity formula to show the representation theoretic existence of Yoshida lifts of
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
.

Remark 1.6
.

The assertion of hypothesis (
Tor
) would be predicted by the Bloch–Kato conjectures, which would relate the finiteness of these Selmer groups to non-vanishing of the Rankin–Selberg
L
-function
L\left(f_{0}\otimes g_{0},\chi_{F},\frac{k_{f_{0}}+k_{g_{0}}}{2}-1\right)
associated to
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
. By combining well-known results of Ramakrishnan
[
72
]
and Jacquet-Shalika
[
49
]
, one can deduce that
L\left(f_{0}\otimes g_{0},\chi_{F},\frac{k_{f_{0}}+k_{g_{0}}}{2}-1\right)\neq 0
. If
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
are CM forms that have complex multiplication by the same imaginary quadratic field
K
, the specialization of the Galois representation
\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}
at
f_{0},g_{0}
splits as a direct sum of two induced characters over
K
. One can then use Rubin’s results
[
76
]
towards the Iwasawa main conjectures over
K
to deduce
Tor
from the non-vanishing of the corresponding
L
-values.

In the case when
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
are both non-CM forms and are non-Galois conjugate twists, one can apply results of
[
50
]
. When the product of the Nebentypen
\chi_{F}^{2}
is non-trivial and the hypothesis labeled Hyp(BI) of
[
50
]
is satisfied, then
[
50
, Theorem 11.6.6]
can be applied to the critical character
\tau=\chi_{F}\epsilon^{\frac{k_{f_{0}}+k_{g_{0}}}{2}-2}
along with
[
50
, Proposition 11.2.9]
to establish
Tor
. To establish the hypothesis (HypBI), one should then appeal to the results
[
57
, Proposition 4.2.1, Remark 4.2.2]
. There are two theoretical limitations to producing a large class of interesting examples in an abstract manner. By interesting, we mean cases where
p
divides the appropriate Rankin-Selberg L-value so as to require congruences between Yoshida lifts and stable forms. Firstly, the results establishing Hyp(BI) in
[
57
, Proposition 4.2.1, Remark 4.2.2]
are asymptotic as they hold for all but finitely many primes. See the conclusion of
[
57
, Theorem 3.2.2]
. Secondly, it is only known conjecturally that 100% of the primes are
ordinary
for general non-CM modular forms. We thus leave it to the interested computational expert to produce numerical examples satisfying Hyp(BI) (thereby guaranteeing
Tor
) along with our hypothesis

p

-Dist-GSp4
given below (and where
p
divides the appropriate Rankin–Selberg
L
-value).

The hypothesis
Yos-Neb
allows us to consider a twist of a specialization of
\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}
, which is essentially self-dual. Let
\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}:\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{4}({\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}})
denote the Galois representation obtained by the action of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
on the following
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
-module of rank four:

(3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}}\bigg(\mathrm{L}_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}}{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}},\ \mathrm{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}\bigg)\otimes_{{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}}{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{\mathcal{F}}\kappa_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}}\right).

Greenberg’s methodology produces a discrete non-primitive Selmer group
\mathrm{Sel}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}}(\mathbb{Q})
, whose Pontryagin dual is conjectured to be finitely generated torsion
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
-module. Hida
[
35
,
38
,
39
]
has constructed a non-primitive
2
-variable Rankin–Selberg
p
-adic
L
-function
\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{3},2}
in the fraction field of
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
. For these
p
-adic
L
-functions, one can vary the weights of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
. We recall the non-primitive variation of the Iwasawa–Greenberg main conjecture associated to
\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}
(
[
26
, Conjecture 4.1]
).

Conjecture 1.7
.

The
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
-module
\mathrm{Sel}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}
is torsion. Furthermore, we have the following equality of diviors of
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
:

(4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee})=\mathrm{Div}(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{3},2}).

More precisely, we introduce an ideal
{\mathcal{C}}
in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
, which we call the
congruence ideal
, and that is expected to satisfy, under a suitable
p
-distinguished hypothesis, the following inequalities of divisors of
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
:

(5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Div}\left(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\geq}}\mathrm{Div}\left({\mathcal{C}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\geq}}\mathrm{Div}\left(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{3},2}\right).

One of our main theorems (Theorem
2
) establishes the following inequality, under suitable assumptions:

\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee})\geq\mathrm{Div}({\mathcal{C}}).

Remark 1.8
.

The formulation of the Iwasawa–Greenberg main conjectures in
[
26
]
relates the primitive Selmer group to the primitive
p
-adic
L
-function, while the formulations in Conjectures
1.7
and
1.3
relate the non-primitive Selmer groups to the non-primitive
p
-adic
L
-functions. The idea that non-primitive versions of the main conjectures should be equivalent to their primitive versions is well-known and goes back to works of Greenberg
[
25
]
, Greenberg–Vatsal
[
34
]
etc. For reasons of space, we do not discuss this equivalence in our paper. However, for some technical reasons, it will be advantageous for us to work with the non-primitive versions. For instance, the theory of Hecke operators at bad primes is not well developed for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
. Although there might be candidates for these Hecke operators at bad places in works of Andrianov
[
3
]
and Roberts–Schmidt
[
75
]
, we prefer to avoid this latter complication altogether. It is for these reasons that we simply concentrate on the
non-primitive main conjecture
and work with the reduced Hecke algebra, thereby not including Hecke operators at bad primes.

1.4.
Hida Theory for Siegel modular forms of genus two

Our starting point is the construction of an automorphic representation, say
\varPi(f_{0},g_{0})
, in the space of Yoshida lifts of
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
. As seen in Section
3
, one can observe that this automorphic representation is
p
-ordinary and has weight

(6)

\displaystyle(k_{1},k_{2})=\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{wt}(f_{0})+\mathrm{wt}(g_{0})}{2},\dfrac{\mathrm{wt}(f_{0})-\mathrm{wt}(g_{0})}{2}+2\right).

We shall impose the following
p
-distinguished hypothesis for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
, which implies and is stronger than the hypothesis

p

-Dist
.

(
p
-Dist-GSP4)

The four residual characters appearing in the restrictions to
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
of the semi-simplification of the residual representation associated to the Yoshida lift
\varPi(f_{0},g_{0})
are distinct.

We follow Hida’s
[
40
]
and Pilloni’s
[
69
]
works for the exposition on Hida Theory for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
over
\mathbb{Q}
. One can consider the local component of the ordinary Hecke algebra with tame level
N
, say
\mathbb{H}(N)_{\mathrm{m}}
, passing through the
p
-adic family of Hecke eigenvalues afforded by
\varPi(f_{0},g_{0})
. For the sake of brevity in the introduction, we denote this ring by
\mathbb{H}_{\mathfrak{m}}
. The Hecke algebra is generated by the
T_{\ell}
,
R_{\ell}
,
S_{\ell}
(and its inverse
S_{\ell}^{-1}
) operators for the good primes
\ell\neq p
, along with the
U_{\mathcal{P}}
,
U_{\mathcal{Q}}
operators. The ring
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}
turns out to be a reduced local ring. Let
\{\eta_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}
denote the set of minimal prime ideals of
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}
. It is a finite extension of the two variable power series ring
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![X_{1},X_{2}]\!]
, where
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are the “weight variables” in the
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
setting. Observe that the intersection of the minimal prime ideals
\displaystyle\bigcap\limits_{i=1}^{m}\eta_{i}
equals
(0)
. Consequently, the natural map
\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}\hookrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{m}{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{i}}
is an injection and the localizations
{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{i}}
are fields. Since
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![X_{1},X_{2}]\!]\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}
is a finite integral extension, we have the following decomposition of
\mathrm{Frac}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![X_{1},X_{2}]\!])
-algebras:

(7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![X_{1},X_{2}]\!]}\mathrm{Frac}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![X_{1},X_{2}]\!])\cong\prod_{i=1}^{m}{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{i}}.

Associated to each minimal prime ideal
\eta_{i}
(also called
branch
or a
Hida family
), one can use the theory of pseudocharacters (for example, see the book by Bellaïche–Chenevier
[
6
]
) to construct a four-dimensional continuous semi-simple Galois representation
\rho_{\eta_{i}}:G_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{4}({(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{i}})
associated to it. The trace of
\rho_{\eta_{i}}
interpolates (in a suitable sense) traces of Galois representations constructed by Laumon
[
53
]
, Taylor
[
81
]
and Weissauer
[
87
]
associated to
p
-ordinary
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
cuspforms.

Definition 1.9
.

We say that the natural map
\phi_{i}:\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}\rightarrow{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{i}}
is said to be the
p
-adic family of Hecke eigensystems associated to the space of Yoshida lifts of
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
if there exists injective ring maps
\iota_{F}:{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}\hookrightarrow{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{i}}
and
\iota_{G}:{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}\hookrightarrow{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{i}}
such that the following equality inside
{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{i}}
holds for all primes
\ell
not dividing
Np
:

\mathrm{Trace}\left(\rho_{\eta_{i}}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell})\right)=\mathrm{Trace}\left(\left(\rho_{\mathcal{G}}(\sqrt{\kappa_{\mathcal{F}}\kappa_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}})\oplus\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\right)(\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell})\right).

The
p
-adic family of Hecke eigenvalues afforded by
\varPi(f_{0},g_{0})
determines a ring homomorphism

\lambda_{f_{0},g_{0}}:\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}.

Let
\eta
denote a(ny) minimal prime ideal of
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}
contained in
\ker(\lambda_{f_{0},g_{0}})
. Without loss of generality, we let the unique minimal prime ideal
\eta
equal
\eta_{1}
. Expanding on the decomposition in equation (
7
), we write

(8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![x_{1},x_{2}]\!]}\mathrm{Frac}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![x_{1},x_{2}]\!])\cong{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{1}}\times\underbrace{{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{2}}\times\cdots\times{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{r}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$p$-adic families}\\
\text{of Hecke eigensystems}\\
\text{not associated to Yoshida lifts}\end{subarray}}\times\underbrace{{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{r+1}}\times{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{m}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Yoshida lift of}\\
\text{ Hida families $\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}$ }\\
\text{ where }(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\neq(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\end{subarray}}.

•

We let
\mathbb{T}
denote the image of
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}}\rightarrow{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{1}}\times\underbrace{{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{2}}\times\cdots\times{(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{m}})}_{\eta_{r}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$p$-adic families}\\
\text{of Hecke eigensystems}\\
\text{not associated to Yoshida lifts}\end{subarray}}
. Note that
\mathbb{T}
is also a local ring.

•

We let
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{Y}}
denote the image of the map
\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\eta}
. The ring
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is a domain. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
denote the integral closure of
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{Y}}
in its fraction field. The kernel of the map
\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\eta}
, which we denote
\mathcal{J}
, will be called the
Yoshida ideal
(following Agarwal–Klosin
[
2
]
).

\mathcal{J}\coloneqq\ker\left(\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\eta}\right).

•

We let
\mathbb{T}_{\perp,\mathrm{stab}}
denote the image of the map

\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\prod\limits_{i\neq 1}\mathbb{T}_{\eta_{i}}.

The set
\{\eta_{i}\}_{2\leq i\leq m}
could be empty, in which case
\mathbb{T}_{\perp,\mathrm{stab}}
would denote the “zero ring”. It is not hard to see that the Yoshida ideal
\mathcal{J}
can naturally be viewed as an ideal of
\mathbb{T}_{\perp,\mathrm{stab}}
.

Definition 1.10
.

The pushout in the category of
\mathbb{T}
-algebras of the following diagram is called the
congruence module
.

\textstyle{\mathbb{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{\perp,\mathrm{stab}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\star}

Despite the terminology “module”, the congruence module is in fact a ring.

(1)

As a
\mathbb{T}_{{\perp,\mathrm{stab}}}
-module, the congruence module is isomorphic to
\mathbb{T}_{\perp,\mathrm{stab}}/\mathcal{J}
.

(2)

As a
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-module, the congruence module is isomorphic to
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{Y}}/\mathcal{C}
, for some ideal
\mathcal{C}
inside
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. By abuse of notation, we will also denote by
{\mathcal{C}}
, the corresponding ideal generated inside the integral closure
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
of
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. The ideal
\mathcal{C}
is called the
congruence ideal
.

Remark 1.11
.

The hypothesis

p

-Dist-GSp4
is stronger than

p

-Dist
and we require the stronger hypothesis for two important reasons. Firstly, it is required to show that the change of basis matrix, used in Theorem
4.6
to establish ordinarity of the local Galois representation, has the required integral structure. Secondly, this hypothesis allows us to work with the strict Selmer group and establish precise control theorems in Section
5.2
, as we avoid the phenomenon of trivial zeroes.

Remark 1.12
.

For our proofs in Section
5
, we will need to choose an auxiliary
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
-tame level
N_{2}
dividing both
N_{\mathcal{F}}
and
N_{\mathcal{G}}
, as given below:

(13)

\displaystyle N_{2}\coloneqq\prod_{\ell\mid N_{\mathcal{F}}N_{\mathcal{G}}}\ell^{\max\left\{2,\mathrm{val}_{\ell}(N_{\mathcal{F}})+2\mathrm{val}_{\ell}(\ell-1),\mathrm{val}_{\ell}(N_{\mathcal{G}})+2\mathrm{val}_{\ell}(\ell-1)\right\}}.

We will also consider Hida’s Hecke algebras
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{F},N_{2}}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{G},N_{2}}
, defined analogous to
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{F},N_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{G},N_{\mathcal{G}}}
, but with tame level
N_{2}
instead. One could have primes
\ell\neq p
dividing
N_{\mathcal{G}}
but not
N_{\mathcal{F}}
. Such primes could contribute to introducing new irreducible components for the ring
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{F},N_{2}}
which were not present in the ring
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{F},N_{\mathcal{F}}}
. For example, one could have primes
\ell\neq p
participating in Ribet’s level raising congruences (even if we work with squarefree levels). For the tame level chosen in equation (
13
), one could also twist the Hida family
\mathcal{F}
by characters with conductor
\ell
and order
p
, if a prime
\ell
dividing
N_{\mathcal{G}}
is also congruent to
1
modulo
p
. For these reasons, one could truly have new Yoshida lifts of Hida families appearing in the decomposition given in equation (
8
).

1.5.
Rigidity results – Existence and Uniqueness: Theorem
1

There are three aspects to our first theorem. Theorem
1
(i)
and
1

(ii)
are existence and uniqueness statements: they establish both the uniqueness of the
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
Hida family passing through the Yoshida lift
\varPi(f_{0},g_{0})
and that every classical specialization of the
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
Hida family passing through
\varPi(f_{0},g_{0})
is also a Yoshida lift; in fact it is obtained as the Yoshida lift of the classical specializations of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
Hida families
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
respectively. Theorem
1
(iii)
establishes that the
4
-dimensional Galois representation associated to the Hida families of
\mathbb{T}_{\perp,\mathrm{stab}}
are irreducible.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose that the hypotheses
Irr
,

p

-Dist-GSp4
,
Mult-Free
,
Tor
,
CT
,
Yos-Det

Yos-disc
and
Yos-Neb
,hold. Then, the following statements hold:

(i)

There is a natural isomorphism
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}\cong\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
of integrally closed domains. With respect to the injections

{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{F}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}\cong\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\eta},\qquad{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{G}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{G}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}\cong\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\eta},

the natural map
\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\eta}
is the
p
-adic family of Hecke eigensystems associated to the space of Yoshida lifts of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
. In other words, there
exists
a
p
-adic family of Hecke eigensystems associated to
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
.

(ii)

\eta
is the unique minimal prime ideal contained in
\ker(\lambda_{f_{0},g_{0}})
. In other words, there exists a
unique

\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
-Hida family passing through the Yoshida lift of
f_{0}
,
g_{0}
.

(iii)

The Galois representation attached to a
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
-Hida family in the component
\mathbb{T}_{\perp,\mathrm{stab}}
is irreducible.

As motivation for the rigidity result of Theorem
1
, one can consider the following question:

•

Suppose that a
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
Hida family
F^{\prime}
passes through a classical CM form (resp. Eisenstein series) with weight
k\geq 2
. Are all classical specializations of
F^{\prime}
CM forms (resp. Eisenstein series)?

This question has an affirmative answer due to Hida. There are two ingredients: (a) Hida
[
36
]
has proved uniqueness of Hida families passing through a classical specialization with classical weight
\geq 2
, and (b) Hida
[
39
]
has constructed
p
-adic families of Eisenstein series and cuspidal CM forms. On the one hand, ingredient (a) in the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
setting relies both on the vertical control theorem of Hida and duality between the space of modular forms and Hecke operators. On the other hand, ingredient (b) uses the fact that one can bound the tame level over a
p
-adic family of CM forms (resp. Eisenstein series) and that one has an explicit description of their Fourier coefficients. Note that these questions need not have an affirmative answer, if one instead considers forms with weight
k=1
.

Since in the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
-setting, the Hecke eigensystem determines the cuspform, one can ask whether there is a purely Galois theoretic approach (that is, without using explicit descriptions of Fourier coefficients) to the above question.

•

Suppose that the Galois representation associated to a
p
-distinguished
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
Hida family
F^{\prime}
at a classical specialization with weight
k\geq 2
is induced from an imaginary quadratic field (respectively reducible). Is the Galois representation associated to all classical specializations of
F^{\prime}
induced from an imaginary quadratic field (respectively reducible)?

Indeed, the idea to study a purely Galois theoretic approach to the above question in the context of Eisenstein series can be traced back to Bellaıche–Chenevier
[
5
]
. The finiteness of class groups comes into play. One can similarly answer this Galois theoretic question in the context of CM forms, when the associated adjoint representation is
p
-distinguished: one has to study an (appropriately twisted) anti-cyclotomic class group of an imaginary quadratic field. One of the novelties in our current work is to establish a similar Galois-theoretic approach in the
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
-setting, without using explicit constructions of
p
-adic families of Yoshida lifts. This is the main motivation for Theorem
1
. The analogous hypothesis in our result, labelled
Tor
, is the finiteness of the Rankin–Selberg Selmer group.

One may ask whether there is a proof simply for the
existence
of the
p
-adic family of Hecke eigensystems associated to the Yoshida lifts of the two
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
Hida families
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
without assuming the finiteness of Selmer groups as in hypothesis
Tor
. In light of the connection between global theta lifts and
L
-values via the Rallis inner product formula (see
[
44
]
), it is expected that an explicit construction of
p
-adic families of the global theta lifts may play an important role in establishing the inequality
\mathrm{Div}({\mathcal{C}})\geq\mathrm{Div}(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{3},2})
of equation (
5
). This is part of ongoing work of the first author and Liu. This is related to establishing the hypotheses
Stab
contingent on the
p
-divisibility of a special value of the normalized Rankin–Selberg complex
L
-function. Nevertheless without a direct automorphic construction of
p
-adic families of Yoshida lifts, one can construct the
p
-adic family of Hecke eigensystems associated to the space of Yoshida lifts of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
if the following premise holds: one has to be able to bound the tame Siegel congruence level for the Yoshida lifts of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
eigenforms
f_{i}
and
g_{j}
, as one varies over the modular forms
f_{i}
and
g_{j}
of the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
Hida families
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, chosen to satisfy the hypotheses given at the beginning of Section
3.2
. Proposition
5.1
lists two hypotheses, labelled
ODDL
and
LR
, which allows such a bounded choice. For the hypothesis
ODDL
, one requires the tame level to be odd. This hypothesis is related to work of Ganapathy
[
22
]
on the preservation of
depth
for primes
\ell\geq 3
under the Local Langlands correspondence for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
along with the relationship between depth and fixed vectors for the Iwahori level. See also Remark
3.7
. A bounded choice of tame level would be guaranteed when the tame levels
N_{\mathcal{F}}
and
N_{\mathcal{G}}
are square free and the Atkin–Lehner eigenvalues coincide at all the primes dividing both
N_{\mathcal{F}}
and
N_{\mathcal{G}}
, as in the works of the first author and Namikawa
[
43
]
along with Saha–Schmidt
[
77
]
. This is hypothesis
LR
. In this case, one can let the
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
tame level
N
to equal
\mathrm{lcm}(N_{\mathcal{F}},N_{\mathcal{G}})
. While Saha–Schmidt analyse the local representations at “bad primes” following Brooks–Schmidt
[
75
]
, the first author and Namikawa
[
43
]
produce an explicit construction of Yoshida lifts using well-chosen test functions at primes
l
dividing
N_{\mathcal{F}}
and
N_{\mathcal{G}}
under these assumptions.

Although vertical control theorems of Hida and Pilloni are available in the
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
setting for
\Lambda
-adic families of modular forms, the absence of duality between the space of modular forms and the Hecke algebra in the
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
setting (in contrast with the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
setting as in Hida’s work
[
37
]
) prevents us from using these control theorems for modular forms to directly establish
uniqueness
of
p
-adic family of Hecke eigensystems.

1.6.
Results towards the Iwasawa–Greenberg main conjectures: Theorem
2
and Corollary
1.13

Theorem 2
.

Suppose that the hypotheses
Irr
,

p

-Dist-GSp4
,
Mult-Free
,
Yos-Det

Yos-disc
and
Yos-Neb
,hold. In addition, suppose that the following hypotheses hold:

(Yos-Exist)

The conclusion of Theorem
1

(i)
holds.

(Stab)

\mathbb{T}_{\perp,\mathrm{stab}}\neq 0
.

Then, we have the following inequality of divisors of
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
:

(14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Div}\left(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}\right)\geq\mathrm{Div}\left({\mathcal{C}}\right).

Under the additional hypotheses
Tor
and
CT
, note that Theorem
1
would establish the hypothesis
Yos-Exist
of Theorem
2
. We prefer working with the hypothesis
Yos-Exist
in Theorem
2
since as stated earlier and illustrated in Proposition
5.1
under the hypothesis
LR
, it is also possible to construct
p
-adic families of Hecke eigensystems of Yoshida lifts.

The Euler system machinery is expected to yield the following (opposite) inequality over
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
for the
3
-variable main conjecture. See works of Lei–Loeffler–Zerbes
[
56
]
, Loeffler–Kings–Zerbes
[
51
,
50
]
and Büyükboduk–Ochiai
[
10
]
for various hypotheses under which such an inequality is available.

\mathrm{Div}\left(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\leq}}\mathrm{Div}\left(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{4},3}\right).

One can use the fact that the direction of the inequality afforded by the Euler system machinery is opposite to the one given by the method of congruences to deduce both Conjectures
1.7
and
1.3
. These ideas fall under the topic of specialization of Selmer groups and are dealt with in an axiomatic manner in the second author’s thesis
[
68
]
. A crucial ingredient in the current setup to these specialization techniques is a result of the second author and Lei
[
59
]
, where they establish that if
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
-module
\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}
is torsion, then the divisor
\mathrm{Div}\left(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}\right)
in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
is principal.

Corollary 1.13
.

In addition to the hypotheses of Theorem
2
, suppose also that the following hypotheses hold:

(1)

We have the following inequality of divisors in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
:

(15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Div}\left(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}\right)\leq\mathrm{Div}(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{4},3}),

(2)

We have the following inequality of divisors in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
:

(16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Div}({\mathcal{C}}){\geq}\mathrm{Div}(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{3},2}).

Then, Conjectures
1.7
and
1.3
hold. That is, we obtain the following equality of divisors in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
:

(17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Div}\left(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}\right)=\mathrm{Div}\left(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{4},3}\right),

along with the following equality of divisors in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}
:

(18)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Div}\left(\mathrm{Sel}^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{3},2}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}\right)=\mathrm{Div}\left({\mathcal{C}}\right)=\mathrm{Div}\left(\theta^{\Sigma_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{3},2}\right).

1.7.
Pseudo-cyclicity of the dual of the primitive Selmer group: Theorem
3

As we mentioned earlier, the Panchishkin condition for the Galois representation
\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}
depends on the choice of a dominant Hida family. We will need to emphasize this point (that there is a dominant Hida family) only for Theorem
3
. Consequently, to state our result in Theorem
3
, we adopt a notation for Selmer groups that is slightly different from our earlier results. We let
\mathrm{Sel}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3},\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}(\mathbb{Q})
and
\mathrm{Sel}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3},\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{G}}}}(\mathbb{Q})
denote the discrete
primitive
Selmer group associated to
\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3}
when
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
respectively are chosen to be dominant. Let
\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{4},3,\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}
and
\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{4},3,\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{G}}}}
respectively denote the principal divisors in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
associated to the Pontraygin duals of the primitive Selmer groups. The fact that these divisors are principal is also shown in
[
59
]
. We will also consider the following hypothesis:

(
GCD)

The height of the ideal
(\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{4},3,\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}},\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{4},3,\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{G}}}})
in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
generated by is at least two.

Theorem 3
.

In addition to the hypotheses of Theorem
2
, suppose that the hypothesis
GCD
holds. Then, the
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
-modules
\mathrm{Sel}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3},\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}
and
\mathrm{Sel}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3},\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{G}}}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}
are pseudo-cyclic. That is, for every height one prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
in
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!]
, we have the following surjections of
({\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!])_{\mathfrak{p}}
-modules:

\displaystyle({\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!])_{\mathfrak{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\left(\mathrm{Sel}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3},\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}},\qquad({\mathbb{I}}_{\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}]\!])_{\mathfrak{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\left(\mathrm{Sel}_{\rho_{\boldsymbol{4},3},\mathbf{\underline{\mathcal{G}}}}(\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}.

Remark 1.14
.

Analogs of the hypothesis
GCD
have been recently considered in the topic of higher codimension Iwasawa theory
[
7
,
58
]
and is related to pseudo-nullity conjectures, following Greenberg
[
27
, Conjecture 3.5]
. See the discussion in
[
59
, Section 1.3]
. We do not consider a similar hypothesis for the pair of non-primitive Selmer groups since they may have a common factor corresponding to the local Euler factors away from
p
.

Notations

We shall use the following notation. Let
J=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}0_{2}&{\mathbf{1}}_{2}\\
-{\mathbf{1}}_{2}&0_{2}\end{array}\right)\in{\rm GL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})
. Let
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
be the symplectic group of degree
4
. For any commutative ring
R
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(R):=\left\{g\in\mathrm{GL}_{4}(R)\mid gJg^{t}=\nu(g)J,\quad\nu(g)\in R^{\times}\right\}.

The map
\nu:\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(R)\rightarrow R^{\times}
is called the similitude character. Denote by
B
the standard Borel subgroup of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
consisting of matrices of the form

\begin{pmatrix}*&*&*&*\\
&*&*&*\\
&&*&\\
&&*&*\end{pmatrix}

For
(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{3}
, put

\big[\,a,\,b;\,c\big]=\begin{pmatrix}a&&&\\
&b&&\\
&&a^{-1}c&\\
&&&b^{-1}c\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{GSp}_{4}.

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}_{f}
denote respectively the adeles and the finite adeles of
\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
\mathrm{I}w_{p}
be the Iwahori subgroup of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, consisting of matrices in
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
whose reduction modulo
p
belongs to the Borel subgroup
B(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})
.

Define a morphism
h:\mathbb{C}^{\times}\to G(\mathbb{R})
by

h(x+\sqrt{-1}y)=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}x1_{2}&y1_{2}\\
-y1_{2}&x1_{2}\end{array}\right).

Let
K_{\infty}
denote the centralizer of
h
in
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{R})
and let
\mathfrak{g}
be the Lie algebra of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{R})
.

2.
Hecke algebras for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}

2.1.
The universal Hecke algebra

Let
A
be a commutative ring. For each prime
\ell
and an open-compact subgroup
K\subset\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
, we let
\mathcal{H}(K,A)
be the ring (multiplication is via convolution), consisting of
K
bi-invariant locally constant functions
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})\rightarrow A
with compact support. If
K
equals
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
, it is well-known that the algebra
\mathcal{H}(K,A)
is commutative and is equal to the polynomial ring

A[T_{\ell},R_{\ell},S_{\ell},S_{\ell}^{-1}],

where
T_{\ell}
,
R_{\ell}
and
S_{\ell}
are respectively the characteristic functions of the following double cosets:

\displaystyle\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\big[\,\ell,\,\ell;\,\ell\big]\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),\quad\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\big[\,\ell^{2},\,\ell;\,\ell^{2}\big]\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),\quad\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\big[\,\ell,\,\ell;\,\ell^{2}\big]\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})

(see
[
70
, Section 5.1.3]
). If
N
is a positive integer, define the universal anemic Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(N,A)
of level
N
by

\mathcal{H}(N,A)=\bigotimes_{\ell\nmid N}^{\prime}\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),A).

Let
A[U_{\mathcal{P}},U_{\mathcal{Q}}]
be the subalgebra of
\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{I}w_{p},A)
, where
U_{\mathcal{P}}
and
U_{\mathcal{Q}}
are the characteristic functions of the following double cosets:

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}w_{p}\big[\,p,\,p;\,p\big]\mathrm{I}w_{p},\qquad\mathrm{I}w_{p}\big[\,p^{2},\,p;\,p^{2}\big]\mathrm{I}w_{p}.

Define the universal Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(N,\mathrm{I}w_{p},A)
of the Iwahori level at
p
by

\mathcal{H}(N,\mathrm{I}w_{p},A):=A[U_{\mathcal{P}},U_{\mathcal{Q}}]\bigotimes^{\prime}_{\ell\nmid Np}\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),A)

It is isomorphic to the polynomial ring

A\left[\left\{U_{\mathcal{P}},U_{\mathcal{Q}}\right\}\cup\left\{T_{\ell},R_{\ell},S_{\ell},S_{\ell}^{-1}\right\}_{\ell\nmid Np}\right]
over the countable set
\left\{U_{\mathcal{P}},U_{\mathcal{Q}}\right\}\cup\left\{T_{\ell},R_{\ell},S_{\ell},S_{\ell}^{-1}\right\}
, as
\ell
variables over primes not dividing
Np
.

2.2.
Hida theory for Siegel modular forms

Let
T^{1}
denote the diagonal torus of
{\rm Sp}_{4}
defined for any commutative ring
R
as follows:

T^{1}(R)=\{\big[\,t_{1},\,t_{2};\,1\big]\mid t_{1},t_{2}\in R^{\times}\}.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
denote the ring of integers in a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Consider the completed group
\mathcal{O}
-algebra:

\widetilde{\Lambda}:=\mathcal{O}\llbracket{T^{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})}\rrbracket.

For each
integer weight

{\underline{k}}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the character

T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times},\quad{\rm diag}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{1}^{-1},t_{2}^{-1})\mapsto t_{1}^{k_{1}}t_{2}^{k_{2}}.

determines a ring homomorphism of completed
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebras:

(19)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\underline{k}}:\widetilde{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

For each weight
{\underline{k}}=(k_{1},k_{2})
, let
P_{\underline{k}}
denote the prime ideal
\ker\varphi_{\underline{k}}
of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
.
Let
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm cls}(\widetilde{\Lambda})
and
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm temp}(\widetilde{\Lambda})
denote the set of classical points and tempered weights (with
k_{2}>3
):

\mathfrak{X}^{\rm cls}(\widetilde{\Lambda}):=\{P_{\underline{k}}\mid k_{1}\geq k_{2}>3\},\qquad\mathfrak{X}^{\rm temp}(\widetilde{\Lambda}):=\{P_{\underline{k}}\mid k_{1}>k_{2}>3\}

For any open-compact subgroup
U\subset\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, denote by
S_{\underline{k}}(U,\mathcal{O})
the space of Siegel modular forms of weight
{\underline{k}}
and level
U
with coefficients in
\mathcal{O}
. Let
N
be a positive integer prime to
p
. Let
K
be an open-compact subgroup of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
such that
K_{\ell}
equals
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
for all primes
\ell\nmid N
. Now consider

K_{0}(p)\coloneqq\{g\in K\mid g_{p}\in\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\}.

Then
S_{\underline{k}}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O})
is a natural
\mathcal{H}(N,\mathrm{I}w_{p},\mathcal{O})
-module.
Let
e_{\rm ord}:=\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(U_{\mathcal{P}}U_{\mathcal{Q}})^{n!}
denote the ordinary projector on
S_{\underline{k}}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O})
. Let
S^{\rm ord}_{\underline{k}}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O})
denote
e_{\rm ord}\cdot S_{\underline{k}}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O})
.

We have the following
vertical control
theorem, independently due to Hida
[
40
, Theorem 1.1]
and Pilloni
[
69
, Theorem 7.1]
.

Theorem 2.1

(Control Theorem)
.

There exists a
\mathcal{H}(N,\mathrm{I}w_{p},\widetilde{\Lambda})
-module
\mathbf{S}^{{\rm ord}}(K)
such that

(1)

\mathbf{S}^{{\rm ord}}(K)
is free
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-module with finite rank,

(2)

For every classical weight
P_{\underline{k}}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\rm cls}(\widetilde{\Lambda})
, we have an
\mathcal{H}(N,\mathrm{I}w_{p},\mathcal{O})
-module isomorphism

\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K)\otimes_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\widetilde{\Lambda}/P_{\underline{k}}\simeq S_{\underline{k}}^{\rm ord}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O}).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{\rm cusp}:=\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{ord}-B,\star}_{\mathrm{cusp}}
be the
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-module introduced in the first paragraph of
[
69
, §7, p.379]
. By construction,
\mathcal{V}_{\rm cusp}
is equipped with a natural Hecke action
\mathcal{H}(N,\mathrm{I}w_{p},\widetilde{\Lambda})\to{\rm End}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(\mathcal{V}_{\rm cusp})
. Let

\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{cusp}},\widetilde{\Lambda}).

By
[
69
, Theorem 7.1(4,6)]
,
\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K)
is a free
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-module and for any weight
{\underline{k}}
,

\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K)/P_{\underline{k}}\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K_{0}(p))\simeq\widehat{S}_{\underline{k}}^{\rm ord}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O}),

where
\widehat{S}_{\underline{k}}^{\rm ord}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O})
is the space of ordinary
p
-adic modular forms of weight
{\underline{k}}=(k_{1},k_{2})
and level
K_{0}(p)
. By
[
69
, Proposition 5.4 and Théorème A.3]
, ordinary
p
-adic modular forms of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
with
k_{1}\geq k_{2}
are overconvergent and by
[
12
, Théorème and Remarque, p.977]
, any overconvergent modular form of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
with
k_{1}\geq k_{2}>3
is classical. We find that

\widehat{S}_{\underline{k}}^{\rm ord}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O})=S_{\underline{k}}^{\rm ord}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O})\text{ if }k_{1}\geq k_{2}>3.

This completes the proof.
∎

Definition 2.2
.

Let
{\rm H}_{{\underline{k}}}^{\mathrm{ord}}(N)
be the
\mathcal{O}
-subalgebra of
{\rm End}_{\mathcal{O}}(S^{{\rm ord}}_{\underline{k}}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O}))
generated by the image of
\mathbb{H}(N)
. We define the big cuspidal Hecke algebra
\mathbb{H}(N)
to be the
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-subalgebra of
{\rm End}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K))
generated by the image of
\mathcal{H}(N,\mathrm{I}w_{p},\widetilde{\Lambda})
. Since
{\rm End}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K))
is finitely generated as
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-module,
\mathbb{H}(N)
is also finitely generated as a
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-module.

Proposition 2.3
.

For every
P_{\underline{k}}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\rm cls}(\widetilde{\Lambda})
, the specialization map

{\rm sp}_{\underline{k}}:\mathbb{H}(N)\otimes_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\widetilde{\Lambda}/P_{{\underline{k}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\rm H}_{{\underline{k}}}^{\mathrm{ord}}(K),\quad t\mapsto t|_{S^{{\rm ord}}_{\underline{k}}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O})}

is surjective and the kernel of
{\rm sp}_{\underline{k}}
is contained in the radical of
\mathbb{H}(N)\otimes\widetilde{\Lambda}/P_{{\underline{k}}}
.

Proof.

The surjectivity is clear. Let
t\in\mathbb{H}(N)
. Since
\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K)
is a free
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-module of finite rank (say
n
), we can consider the characteristic polynomial
Q(X)\in\widetilde{\Lambda}[X]
of
t
. The reduction
Q(X)\pmod{P_{{\underline{k}}}}
is the characteristic polynomial of its image under
{\rm sp}_{{\underline{k}}(t)}
. If
t
belongs to
\ker{\rm sp}_{\underline{k}}
, then
Q(X)\pmod{P_{{\underline{k}}}}=X^{n}
. We can thus write
Q(X)=X^{n}+\beta(X)
, for some element
\beta(X)\in P_{{\underline{k}}}[X]
. It follows by using the Cayley-Hamilton theorem that
t^{n}+\beta(t)
, viewed as an element of
\mathbb{H}(N)
, equals
0
. Since
\beta(t)
belongs to
P_{{\underline{k}}}\mathbb{H}(N)
, we can conclude that
t
is nilpotent in
\mathbb{H}(N)/P_{{\underline{k}}}
.
∎

As an immediate corollary to Proposition
2.3
, we have:

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
P_{\underline{k}}
be an element of
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm cls}(\widetilde{\Lambda})
. Then, every ring homomorphism
\mathbb{H}(N)\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
containing the prime ideal
P_{\underline{k}}
of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
corresponds to a
p
-adic Hecke eigensystem with weight
{\underline{k}}
.

Let
\Delta
be the torsion subgroup of
T^{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and let
\widehat{\Delta}=\mathrm{Hom}(\Delta,\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})
be the set of characters of
\Delta
. For each
\vartheta\in\widehat{\Delta}
, define the idempotent

e_{\vartheta}:=\frac{1}{\sharp\Delta}\sum_{\delta\in\Delta}\vartheta(\delta^{-1})[\delta]\in\widetilde{\Lambda}.

Define

\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K,\vartheta):=e_{\vartheta}\cdot\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K);\quad\mathbb{H}(N,\vartheta)=e_{\vartheta}\cdot\mathbb{H}(N).

Let
\Gamma\subset T^{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
be the subgroup defined by

\Gamma:=\{\big[\,t_{1},\,t_{2};\,1\big]\mid t_{1},t_{2}\in 1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}\subset T^{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Let
\Lambda
denote the complete group algebra
\mathcal{O}\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket
of
\Gamma
over
\mathcal{O}
. Then,
\Lambda
turns out to be an Iwasawa algebra, being (non-canonically) isomorphic to the two-variable power series
\mathcal{O}\llbracket{X_{1},X_{2}}\rrbracket
. We also have the following decomposition:

(20)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}=\prod_{\vartheta\in\widehat{\Delta}}\Lambda e_{\vartheta}.

If the character component
\vartheta
is clear from the context, we simply write
\Lambda
instead of
\Lambda e_{\vartheta}
. Each maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
determines a unique
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{m}}\in\widehat{\Delta}
such that

\mathbb{H}(N,\vartheta)_{\mathfrak{m}}=e_{\vartheta_{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathbb{H}(N)_{\mathfrak{m}},

and there exists
(a,b)\in(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{2}
such that
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{m}}(\big[\,t_{1},\,t_{2};\,1\big])=t_{1}^{a}t_{2}^{b}
. Put

\mathfrak{X}^{\rm cls}_{\vartheta}(\Lambda):=\{P_{\underline{k}}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\rm cls}\mid k_{1}\equiv a,\,k_{2}\equiv b\pmod{p}\},\qquad\mathfrak{X}^{\rm temp}_{\vartheta}:=\mathfrak{X}^{\rm cls}_{\vartheta}\cap\mathfrak{X}^{\rm temp}.

Using Theorem
2.1
, one can conclude that
\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
is free of finite rank over
\Lambda
and

\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes\Lambda/P_{{\underline{k}}}\simeq\mathbf{S}_{\underline{k}}^{\rm ord}(K,\mathcal{O}),\quad{\underline{k}}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\rm cls}_{\mathfrak{m}}.

Proposition 2.5
.

The Hecke algebra
\mathbb{H}(N)_{\mathfrak{m}}
is reduced local
\Lambda
-algebra.

Proof.

Let
t\in\mathbb{H}(N)_{\mathfrak{m}}\subset{\rm End}_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{S}^{\rm ord}(K))\simeq\rm M_{n}(\Lambda)
be a nilpotent element with
t^{m}=0
. We have the semisimplicity of the ordinary Hecke algebra
{\rm H}_{{\underline{k}}}^{\mathrm{ord}}(N)
for a fixed weight
{\underline{k}}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\rm temp}
. See
[
41
, Corollary 5.4]
. It thus follows that the image of
t
in
\rm M_{n}(\Lambda/P_{{\underline{k}}})
equals zero for all tempered weights
{\underline{k}}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\rm temp}
. It follows that
t=0
by the Zariski density of
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\rm temp}
. See Lemma
A.1
.
∎

3.
Automorphic representations on
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
and associated Galois representations

3.1.
Automorphic representations

Let
\varPi
be an irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{A})
with the central character
\omega_{\varPi}
. We decompose
\varPi
into the restricted tensor product of
(\mathfrak{g},K_{\infty})
-module
\varPi_{\infty}
and irreducible admissible representations of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
:

\displaystyle\varPi\cong\varPi_{\infty}\otimes\left(\bigotimes\limits_{\ell\text{ finite}}\varPi_{\ell}\right)

For any place
\ell
of
\mathbb{Q}
, the representation
\varPi_{\ell}
is called the local component of
\varPi
at
\ell
. Let
K\coloneqq\prod_{\ell}K_{\ell}\subset\mathrm{GSp}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
be an open-compact subgroup such that
K_{\ell}=\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
for all primes
\ell\nmid N
. Suppose that the finite dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
\varPi^{K}\neq 0
. Then

\varPi^{K}
is a
\mathcal{H}(N,\mathbb{C})
-module. In particular, we obtain a ring homomorphism:

(21)

\phi_{\varPi}:\mathcal{H}(N,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

such that
Tv=\phi_{\varPi}(T)v
for every element
T
in
\mathcal{H}(N,\mathbb{Z})
and every vector
v
in
\varPi^{K}
. This homomorphism
\phi_{\varPi}
is called the Hecke eigensystem associated with
\varPi
.

Let
(k_{1},k_{2})
be integers with

(22)

k_{1}\geq k_{2}\geq 3.

We say that the archimedean component
\varPi_{\infty}
is a holomorphic discrete series of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
if
\varPi_{\infty}\otimes\|\cdot\|^{\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}-3}{2}}
is the unitary holomorphic discrete series representation of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
, i.e.
\varPi_{\infty}
has Harish-Chandra parameter
(k_{1}-1,k_{2}-2)
.
Then
\varPi_{\infty}
is cohomological and
\omega_{\varPi}\|\cdot\|^{k_{1}+k_{2}-3}
is unitary. It is well-known (using results of Chai–Faltings
[
21
]
)
that there exists a number field
E_{\varPi}
such that
\phi_{\varPi}(\mathcal{H}(N,\mathbb{Z}))
lies in its ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{E_{\varPi}}
.
For each
\ell\nmid N
, the abstract Hecke polynomial
Q_{\ell}(X)\in\mathcal{H}(N,\mathbb{Z})[X]
at
\ell
is defined as follows:

(23)

\displaystyle Q_{\ell}(X):=X^{4}-T_{\ell}X^{3}+\ell(R_{\ell}+(\ell^{2}+1)S_{\ell})X^{2}-\ell^{3}T_{\ell}S_{\ell}X+\ell^{6}S_{\ell}^{2}.

Let
Q_{\varPi_{\ell}}(X):=\phi_{\pi}(Q_{\ell}(X))\in\mathcal{O}_{E_{\varPi}}[X]
.

Theorem 3.1
.

[Laumon
[
53
]
, Taylor
[
81
]
, Weissauer
[
87
]
]

Let
\varPi
be a cuspidal cohomological automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{A})
with
\varPi_{\infty}
holomorphic discrete series of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
and
k_{1}>k_{2}\geq 3
.

(1)

Let
E_{\varPi}
denote the field obtained by adjoining to
\mathbb{Q}
, all roots of the Hecke polynomial
Q_{\varPi_{\ell}}(X)
for every finite place
\ell\not\in{\rm Ram}(\varPi)
. Then,
E_{\varPi}
is a number field.

(2)

Let
\eta
be a finite place. There exists a unique continuous semi-simple Galois representation
\varrho_{\varPi,\eta}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{4}(E_{\varPi,\eta})
such that for every finite place
\ell\notin\rm Ram(\varPi)\cup\{\eta\}
, we have the following equality of characteristic polynomials:

\displaystyle\det\left(X\cdot{\mathbf{1}}_{4}-\varrho_{\varPi,\eta}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell})\right)=Q_{\varPi_{\ell}}(X).

Here,
E_{\varPi,\eta}
denotes the compositum of the fields
E_{\varPi}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{\eta}
inside
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\eta}
. In particular, the Galois representation
\varrho_{\varPi,\eta}
is unramified at all primes
\ell\notin\rm Ram(\varPi)\cup\{\eta\}
.

(3)

The Galois representation
\varrho_{\varPi,\eta}
is Hodge–Tate with weights (we normalize the
\eta
-adic cylotomic character to have Hodge–Tate weight
1
)

\displaystyle 0,\quad k_{2}-2,\quad k_{1}-1,\quad k_{1}+k_{2}-3.

Proof.

See
[
60
, Theorem 10.1.3]
.∎

Following the notation in
[
33
, Definition 2.5.2]
, the Arthur parameter
\psi_{\varPi}
can be written as a formal sum
\boxplus_{i=1}^{r}\pi_{i}[d_{i}]
, where
\pi_{i}
are
\omega_{\varPi}
-self dual automorphic representations for
{\rm GL}_{N_{i}}
and
d_{i}
are positive integers such that
\sum_{i=1}^{r}N_{i}d_{i}=4
. Based on Arthur’s work, there are six types of Arthur parameters indexed by (a)-(f) in
[
33
, Remark 6.1.8]
. We say
\varPi
is stable if
\psi_{\varPi}=\pi
, where
\pi
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
{\rm GL}_{4}(\mathbb{A})
and
\varPi
is of Yoshida type if
\psi_{\varPi}=\pi_{1}\boxplus\pi_{2}
, where
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are cuspidal automorphic representations of
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A})
with the same central character and
\pi_{1}\not\simeq\pi_{2}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\varPi
be a cohomological cohomological automorphic representation of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
. Then

(1)

\varPi
is either stable or of Yoshida type if
\varPi
is tempered.

(2)

\varPi
is tempered if
k_{1}>k_{2}\geq 3
.

Proof.

Let
\psi_{\varPi}=\boxplus_{i}\pi_{i}[d_{i}]
be the Arthur parameter of
\varPi
. The Ramanujan conjecture for Siegel modular forms is known due to work of Weissauer
[
88
]
. As a result,
\varPi
is tempered if and only if
d_{i}=1
for all
i
. In view of the classification
[
33
, Remark 6.18]
, we find that
\varPi
is tempered if
\varPi
is stable or of Yoshida type (types (a) and (b)) and is non-tempered for types (c)-(f). For (2), the inspection on the archimedean
L
-parameter rules out the possibility for types (c)-(f).
∎

3.2.
Yoshida lifts

Cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{A})
of
Yoshida type
(as in Arthur’s classification
[
4
]
) are generated by so-called Yoshida lifts.
The original constructions of Yoshida lifts by Yoshida and the generalization by Böcherer–Schulze-Pillot involve producing explicit automorphic forms utilizing the theory of global theta correspondences. Let
S_{\kappa}^{\rm new}(N,\psi)
be the set of cuspidal elliptic newforms of weight
\kappa
, level
\Gamma_{1}(N)
and nebentypus
\psi
.
For a newform
f=\sum_{n>0}a_{n}(f)q^{n}\in S_{\kappa}^{\rm new}(N,\psi)
, we let
\pi_{f}
denote the associated cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q})
. Then the central character of
\pi_{f}
is given by
\psi^{-1}\|\cdot\|^{\frac{1-\kappa}{2}}
, whereas on the other hand,
\pi_{f,\infty}\|\cdot\|^{\frac{\kappa-1}{2}}
is a unitary discrete series representation of weight
\kappa
. Let

\varrho_{f,p}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})

be the
p
-adic Galois representation associated to
f
constructed by Shimura and Deligne. The Galois representations
\rho_{f,p}
is unramified outside
pN
and for any prime
\ell\nmid N_{f}N_{g}\eta
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Trace}(\rho_{f,\eta}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell}))=a_{\ell}(f).

Now we let

(f_{1},f_{2})\in S_{\kappa_{1}}^{\rm new}(N_{1},\psi_{1})\times S_{\kappa_{2}}^{\rm new}(N_{2},\psi_{2})

be a pair of elliptic newforms with
\kappa_{1}\geq\kappa_{2}\geq 2
. Decompose
\pi_{f}
and
\pi_{g}
into the restricted tensor products

\displaystyle\pi_{f_{1}}\cong\pi_{f_{1},\infty}\bigotimes\pi_{f_{1},\ell},\quad\pi_{f_{2}}\cong\pi_{f_{2},\infty}\bigotimes\pi_{f_{2},\ell}.

Consider the following set:

S_{\mathrm{disc},f_{1},f_{2}}=\left\{\text{ finite prime }\ell\mid\pi_{f_{1},\ell},\ \pi_{f_{2},\ell}\text{ are discrete series}\right\}.

We consider the following hypotheses on
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
:

Hypothesis 3.3
.

f_{1}\neq f_{2}
.

Hypothesis 3.4
.

\kappa_{1}\equiv\kappa_{2}\pmod{2}
.

Hypothesis 3.5
.

The set
S_{\mathrm{disc},f_{1},f_{2}}
is non-empty.

Hypothesis 3.6
.

\psi_{1}\psi_{2}^{-1}=\chi^{2}
for some Dirichlet character
\chi
.

By Arthur’s multiplicity formula as established in
[
33
]
(or utilising the representation theoretic approach of Roberts
[
73
, Theorem 8.6
(2)]
), there exists a cuspidal automorphic representation
\varPi
of Yoshida type such that

\psi_{\varPi}=\pi_{f_{1}}\boxplus\pi_{f_{2}}\otimes\|\cdot\|^{\frac{\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{1}}{2}},

and the archimedean component
\varPi_{\infty}
is a discrete series of weight
(\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}}{2},\frac{\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}}{2}+2)
. Moreover every a cuspidal automorphic representation
\varPi
of Yoshida type with
\varPi_{\infty}
a discrete series of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
arises in this way. The
p
-adic Galois representation associated to
\varPi
in Theorem
3.1
is given by

\varrho_{\varPi,p}\cong\varrho_{f_{1},p}\oplus\varrho_{f_{2},p}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}}{2}}\right),

where
\varepsilon
is the
p
-adic cyclotomic character.

Remark 3.7
.

Since the principal congruence subgroups form a basis of neighborhood of the identity element in
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
, to obtain vectors fixed by the principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma^{(2)}(N)
, one simply has to choose high enough powers of the primes
\ell
dividing
N_{f}N_{g}
.

To have some optimal control on the tame level (away from the prime
l=2
), one can use Ganapathy’s result
[
23
, Proposition 9.2]
that the local Langlands correspondence for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
proved by Gan–Takeda
[
32
]
preserves
depth
for primes
l\geq 3
. The Local Langlands correspondence for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
also preserves depth (
[
1
]
). It will allow one to work with the following tame level(if
2
is a ramified prime, one needs to choose a large enough
s
):

(24)

\displaystyle 2^{s}\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\mid N_{F}N_{G}\\
l\neq 2\end{subarray}}l^{\max\{2,\mathrm{val}_{p}(N_{F})+1,\mathrm{val}_{p}(N_{G})+1\}},\qquad s\gg 0

For the relationship between the depth and fixed vectors for the Iwahori level, see
[
23
, Lemma 7.2]
. This representation theoretic approach introduces an extra factor of
+1
in the exponents of primes in the tame level.

3.3.
p
-ordinary automorphic representations

Let
\varPi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{A})
with
\varPi_{\infty}
a discrete series of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
and
k_{1}\geq k_{2}\geq 3
. Suppose that
p\nmid N
. Having fixed an embedding
E_{\varPi}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, we let
\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
be the roots of
Q_{\varPi_{\ell}}(X)
such that

\mathrm{val}_{p}(\alpha_{0})\leq\mathrm{val}_{p}(\alpha_{1})\leq\mathrm{val}_{p}(\alpha_{2})\leq\mathrm{val}_{p}(\alpha_{3}).

We say
\varPi
is
p
-ordinary if

(25)

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}_{p}(\alpha_{0})=0,\qquad\mathrm{val}_{p}(\alpha_{1})=k_{2}-2,\qquad\mathrm{val}_{p}(\alpha_{2})=k_{1}-1,\qquad\mathrm{val}_{p}(\alpha_{3})=k_{1}+k_{2}-3.

Proposition 3.8
.

Suppose that
\varPi
is of Yoshida type with
\psi_{\varPi}=\pi_{1}\boxplus\pi_{2}
and
\varPi_{p}
is spherical. Then
\varPi
is
p
-ordinary if and only if
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are
p
-ordinary.

Proof.

Let
c=\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}-3}{2}
. Since
\varPi_{\infty}
is a discrete series,
\pi_{1,\infty}\|\cdot\|^{c}
and
\pi_{2,\infty}\|\cdot\|^{c}
are unitary discrete series of weights
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
respectively. From the comparison between the archimedean
L
-parameters of
\varPi
and
\pi_{1}\boxplus\pi_{2}
, we find that

\{k_{1}+k_{2}-3,k_{1}-1,k_{2}-2,0\}=\{a_{1}+c,a_{2}+c,-a_{2}+c,-a_{1}+c\}.

On the other hand, Since
p\nmid N
,
\varPi_{p}
is spherical, and hence the representations
\pi_{1}
,
\pi_{2}
are unramified at
p
. Let
(b_{1}+c,-b_{1}+c)
and
(b_{2}+c,-b_{2}+c)
with
0\leq b_{i}\leq c
be the
p
-adic valuations of the Satake parameters at
p
for
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
respectively.
It is clear that if
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are
p
-ordinary, then
a_{i}=b_{i}
and
\varPi
is
p
-ordinary.
Conversely, suppose that
\varPi
is
p
-ordinary. We may assume
a_{1}\geq a_{2}>0
. Since
k_{1}>k_{2}>3
, we obtain

a_{1}=c=\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}-3}{2};\quad a_{2}=\frac{k_{1}-k_{2}+1}{2}.

The
p
-ordinary assumption tells us

\{k_{1}+k_{2}-3,k_{1}-1,k_{2}-2,0\}=\{b_{1}+c,b_{2}+c,-b_{2}+c,-b_{1}+c\}.

Since
-b_{1}+c\leq-a_{1}+c
and
-b_{2}+c\leq-a_{2}+c
, we conclude that
a_{1}=b_{1}
and
a_{2}=b_{2}
. This shows that
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are also
p
-ordinary.
∎

Theorem 3.9

(Urban)
.

Suppose that
\varPi
is
p
-ordinary. Then there exists a matrix
C
in
\mathrm{GL}_{4}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
and four
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
-valued characters
\psi_{0},\ \psi_{1},\ \psi_{2},\ \psi_{3}
unramified at
p
, such that we have the following equality of matrices in
\mathrm{GL}_{4}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
:

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varPi,p}(\sigma)=C\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}\psi_{3}\varepsilon^{k_{1}+k_{2}-3}(\sigma)&\star&\star&\star\\
0&\psi_{2}\varepsilon^{k_{1}-1}(\sigma)&\star&\star\\
0&0&\psi_{1}\varepsilon^{k_{2}-2}(\sigma)&\star\\
0&0&0&\psi_{0}(\sigma)\end{array}\right]C^{-1},\qquad\forall\sigma\in\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Following the notations in equation (
25
), the four unramified characters
\psi_{0},\ \psi_{1},\ \psi_{2},\ \psi_{3}
are determined by the following property:

(30)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=\alpha_{0},\quad\psi_{1}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=\dfrac{\alpha_{1}}{p^{k_{2}-2}},\quad\psi_{2}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=\dfrac{\alpha_{2}}{p^{k_{1}-1}},\quad\psi_{3}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=\dfrac{\alpha_{3}}{p^{k_{1}+k_{2}-3}}.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.2
,
\varPi
is either stable or of Yoshida type. If
\varPi
is stable, the theorem follows from
[
85
, Theorem1, Corollary 1(iii)]
. Suppose that
\varPi
is of Yoshida type with
\psi_{\varPi}=\pi_{1}\boxplus\pi_{2}
. We have
\rho_{\varPi,p}=\rho_{\pi_{1},p}\oplus\rho_{\pi_{2},p}
, where
\rho_{\pi_{i},p}
are the
p
-adic Galois representations associated to
\pi_{i}
for
i=1,2
. By Proposition
3.8
,
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are
p
-ordinary, so the associated Galois representations
\rho_{\pi,p}
are also ordinary at
p
by results of Deligne and Mazur–Wiles (see
[
42
, Theorem 4.2.7]
). Namely
\rho_{\pi_{i},p}
restricted to
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
must be upper-triangular for
i=1,2
. Up to a choice of basis over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, the Galois representation associated to
\varPi
must then have the following shape:

\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}\psi_{3}\varepsilon^{k_{1}+k_{2}-3}&\star&0&0\\
0&\psi_{0}&0&0\\
0&0&\psi_{2}\varepsilon^{k_{1}-1}&\star\\
0&0&0&\psi_{1}\varepsilon^{k_{2}-2}\end{array}\right].

The theorem follows immediately.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

The contragradient
(\varrho_{\eta}^{-1})^{T}
of the Galois representation
\varrho_{\eta}
is the Galois representation appearing in Urban’s work
[
85
]
, where the characteristic polynomial of
\mathrm{Frob}_{\nu}^{-1}
(the geometric Frobenius) is equated to the Hecke polynomial
Q_{\varPi_{\nu}}(X)
. In our statements, we have used the arithmetic Frobenius.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
{\underline{k}}=(k_{1},k_{2})
with
k_{1}>k_{2}>3
. Any cuspidal automorphic representation generated by elements in
S_{\underline{k}}^{\rm ord}(K_{0}(p),\mathcal{O})
must be spherical at
p
.

Proof.

We have a decomposition of
\mathcal{H}(N,\mathrm{I}w_{p},\mathbb{C})
-modules

(31)

S_{\underline{k}}(K_{0}(p),\mathbb{C})=\bigoplus_{\varPi}m_{\varPi}\varPi_{f}^{K_{0}(p)},

where
\varPi
runs over cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbb{A})
with cohomological weight
{\underline{k}}
and
\varPi_{f}^{K_{0}(p)}\neq 0
. We claim that if
\varPi
is ordinary, then
\varPi_{p}
must be spherical. To see the claim, we note that according to the classification of Iwahori-spherical representations at non-archimedean places in
[
75
, Table A13, page 293]
, By the ordinary hypothesis, the
p
-adic valuations of the Satake parameters of
\varPi_{p}
is given by

a_{1}=0\leq a_{2}=k_{2}-2\leq a_{3}=k_{1}-1\leq a_{4}=k_{1}+k_{2}-3.

We find that
a_{i+1}-a_{i}>1
for all
i=1,2,3
.
It follows from the
L
-parameters of representations of Type II-VI in
[
75
, page 53-55]
that
\varPi_{p}
must be of Type I, and hence
\varPi_{p}
is spherical.
∎

3.4.
Hecke eigensystems associated with
p
-ordinary representations

We retain the notation in the previous subsection. Let
\varPi
be an
p
-ordinary automorphic representations with
\varPi^{K}\neq 0
.
There exists a non-zero eigenvector
v_{\rm ord}\in\varPi^{K_{0}(p)}
of
U_{\mathcal{P}}
and
U_{\mathcal{Q}}
such that

\displaystyle U_{\mathcal{P}}v_{\rm ord}=\alpha_{0}v_{\rm ord};\quad U_{\mathcal{Q}}v_{\rm ord}=p^{2-k_{2}}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0}v_{\rm ord}.

This allows us to define the Hecke eigensystem
\phi_{\varPi}^{\rm ord}:\mathcal{H}(N,\mathrm{I}w_{p},\mathbb{Z})\to\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
by

\displaystyle\phi_{\varPi}^{\rm ord}(T)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\varPi}(T),\qquad\forall\ T\in\mathcal{H}(Np),

\displaystyle\phi_{\varPi}^{\rm ord}(U_{\mathcal{P}})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{0},\quad\phi_{p}^{\rm ord}(U_{\mathcal{Q}})=p^{2-k_{2}}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0}.

If
\varPi
is of Yoshida lift with
\psi_{\varPi}=\pi_{f_{1}}\boxplus\pi_{f_{2}}\otimes\|\cdot\|^{\frac{\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{1}}{2}}
for a pair of elliptic newforms
(f_{1},f_{2})
. For
i=1,2
, let
a_{p}(f_{i}^{\dagger})
be the
p
-adic unit root of the Hecke polynomial
X^{2}-a_{p}(f_{i})X+\psi(p)p^{\kappa_{i}-1}
. Then we have

(32)

\displaystyle\phi_{\varPi}^{\rm ord}(Q_{\ell}(X))

\displaystyle=(X^{2}-a_{\ell}(f_{1})X+\psi_{1}(\ell)\ell^{\kappa_{1}-1})(X^{2}-a_{\ell}(f_{2})\ell^{\frac{\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}}{2}}\chi(\ell)X+\psi_{1}(\ell)\ell^{\kappa_{1}-1}),

\displaystyle\phi_{\varPi}^{\rm ord}(U_{\mathcal{P}})

\displaystyle=a_{p}(f_{1}^{\dagger});\quad\phi_{\varPi}(U_{\mathcal{Q}})=a_{p}(f_{1}^{\dagger})a_{p}(f_{2}^{\dagger}).

4.
Pseudocharacters and Generalized Matrix Algebras

Let
S
denote the set of primes dividing
Np
. Let
\mathbb{Q}_{S}
denote the maximal extension of
\mathbb{Q}
unramified outside
S
and
\infty
. Let
G_{S}
denote
\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{S}/\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\mathcal{R}
be a finitely generated
\Lambda
-algebra. Let
n
be a positive integer such that
n!
is invertible in
\mathcal{R}
. We will consider a
pseudocharacter of dimension
n
(following Taylor
[
80
]
and Bellaïche–Chenevier
[
6
]
), which is a function

\mathrm{T}:G_{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}.

Furthermore, we will say that the pseudocharacter is said to be
continuous
if the map
\mathrm{T}:G_{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
is continuous. The map
\mathrm{T}
extends to an
\mathcal{R}
-linear map
\mathcal{R}[G_{S}]\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
which is also a
pseudocharacter
in the language of
[
6
